        "markdown": "[Skip to content](https://wortheverymile.com/en/africa/egypt/travel-guide-for-cairo/#content)\n\n# Cairo, Egypt: The ultimate first-timer’s Travel Guide\n\n- [Africa](https://wortheverymile.com/en/category/africa/), [Egypt](https://wortheverymile.com/en/category/africa/egypt/)\n\n#### Table of Contents\n\n- [Transportation](https://wortheverymile.com/en/africa/egypt/travel-guide-for-cairo/#elementor-toc__heading-anchor-0)\n\n- [Best things to do in Cairo](https://wortheverymile.com/en/africa/egypt/travel-guide-for-cairo/#elementor-toc__heading-anchor-1)\n\n- [Where to eat in Cairo](https://wortheverymile.com/en/africa/egypt/travel-guide-for-cairo/#elementor-toc__heading-anchor-2)\n\n- [When to go](https://wortheverymile.com/en/africa/egypt/travel-guide-for-cairo/#elementor-toc__heading-anchor-3)\n\n- [Useful Sites & Apps](https://wortheverymile.com/en/africa/egypt/travel-guide-for-cairo/#elementor-toc__heading-anchor-4)\n\n- [Cairo, Egypt FAQ — Everything you need to know](https://wortheverymile.com/en/africa/egypt/travel-guide-for-cairo/#elementor-toc__heading-anchor-5)\n\n\nCairo, Egypt, is the largest city in Africa and the Arab world, and one of the most overwhelming, fascinating, and unforgettable cities on the planet. The ‘City of a Thousand Minarets’ sits where ancient and modern Egypt collide: within the city limits lie the Giza Pyramids, the Sphinx, and now the world’s largest museum dedicated to a single civilization, the Grand Egyptian Museum.\n\nThose were the reasons we were attracted and decided to organize our first trip outside Europe, aiming for a special experience in Cairo. Our full travel guide below will provide you with everything you need to know and do on your first visit to Cairo.\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| **Country** | Egypt |\n| **City population** | ~9.8 million (Greater Cairo: ~23 million) |\n| **Nickname** | “City of a Thousand Minarets” / “Mother of the World” |\n| **Time zone** | EET — UTC+2 (no daylight saving) |\n| **Best time to visit** | October–April |\n| **Airport** | Cairo International Airport (CAI) — largest in Egypt |\n| **Metro** | 3 lines — the oldest metro in Africa (since 1987) |\n| **Visa** | Required — 👉 **[iVisa.com](https://www.ivisa.com/?utm_source=wortheverymile)** |\n| **Tours & activities** | 👉 [**GetYourGuide — Cairo**](https://www.getyourguide.com/cairo-l92/?partner_id=85CKKC8&utm_medium=affiliate&placement=button-cta&cmp=cairo) |\n| **Accommodation** | 👉 [**Booking.com — Cairo**](https://www.booking.com/searchresults.el.html?ss=Cairo&label=gen173nr-10CAEoggI46AdIM1gEaFyIAQGYATO4ARfIAQzYAQPoAQH4AQGIAgGoAgG4Ava_qM4GwAIB0gIkNGE5N2RjYjYtYTMyMS00ZTJmLWFlNjEtZTExNTllZGRiYjU02AIB4AIB&sid=ba81ec935d2f2e2ac1b72dc29a634f78&aid=304142&lang=el&sb=1&src_elem=sb&src=index&dest_id=-290692&dest_type=city&ac_position=0&ac_click_type=b&ac_langcode=en&ac_suggestion_list_length=5&search_selected=true&search_pageview_id=27a35ce488080253&ac_meta=GhAyN2EzNWNlNDg4MDgwMjUzIAAoATICZW46BUNhaXJv&group_adults=2&no_rooms=1&group_children=0) |\n\nFor more information, read our **[ultimate travel guide for Egypt](https://wortheverymile.com/en/africa/egypt/travel-guide-for-egypt/)**\n\n## Transportation\n\n##### **From the airport**\n\n**VIP Cars:** At the exits, you will find 7-seat minivans that will take you to your accommodation for a predetermined price.\n\n**Taxi:** You can get black-and-white taxis at a fixed fare of 50-80 EGP, or newer white taxis equipped with a taximeter. However, certain drivers might set a starting price above 100 EGP because they know some tourists negotiate.\n\nCheck prices on **[Welcome Pickups](https://www.welcomepickups.com/?tap_a=26297-438366&tap_s=3089898-02f59c)**\n\n**Uber:** The well-known app will free you from haggling; you will already know the price of the ride and feel more secure, as you can track your journey’s route.\n\n**Bus:** Bus 356 connects Terminal 1 (international flights) to the city center. However, it is not preferred as the timetables are not respected, and there are delays.\n\nIt is a 45-minute distance depending on the traffic. To secure your journey from the airport, we suggest booking a hotel that includes airport transportation as part of its services. In any other occasion, the experience of finding and negotiating a price with one of the many drivers would feel chaotic and frustrating.\n\n##### **Transportation within the city**\n\n**Underground Metro:** 3 lines covering the city. The oldest metro in Africa (opened 1987), ranking among the 15 busiest globally. Fares: EGP 5–10 depending on distance (~$0.30). Cheap and efficient for the main city areas.\n\nTo get to the Pyramids from downtown: Take Metro Line 2 to Giza station (6 stops from Tahrir, ~EGP 6), then take a shared microbus or taxi for the final stretch.\n\n**Tok Tok:** You will have to negotiate and price as there is no meter on the tricycle, but you will experience its vibrant adventure.\n\n**Taxi:** We do not recommend using a taxi within the city. You will find it challenging to communicate with the drivers, as most speak little to no English. The times we took the taxi in Cairo, we showed the address to the driver, and he detoured, forcing the price of the meter to go up (whereas some were not even using the meter), and at the end, they asked for double the meter price, providing us with less change at the end.\n\nUber is the safest and most transparent taxi option in Cairo, with prices set in advance and routes tracked. Avoid street taxis, which often overcharge tourists. A 30-minute journey from Downtown to Khan el-Khalili costs approximately EGP 100–150 by Uber.\n\n**VIP Transport:** Private transport with a driver or tourist guide, depending on the package. Many hotels offer organized tours with a driver or tourist guide. However, some hotels act as intermediaries with local drivers and take a commission. For this reason, make sure to research the prices of the tours you desire.\n\n## Best things to do in Cairo\n\n##### **Egyptian Museum**\n\nIt is considered one of the richest museums in terms of the number of its exhibits, which are among the greatest of pharaonic Egypt. It has 120,000 exhibits, including papyri, jewelry, coins, statues, and personal items of pharaohs discovered during burial excavations. The most important excavation in global history was the excavation of King Tutankhamun’s tomb. Among the findings, an 11-kilo mask stands out and is one of the most important exhibits in the museum. The mummies of the kings impress you (e.g. Ramses III, Amenhotep III, Sneferu, Nefertiti), which are found in very good condition. Be aware that photography is not allowed for some exhibits, and you might be fined if you do so. Also, in 2020, the Grand Egyptian Museum opened, housing around 50,000 exhibits. Some of which moved from the Egyptian Museum.\n\n![Egyptian museum at Cairo](https://wortheverymile.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/03/G0094300-1.jpg.webp)\n\n##### **Giza Necropolis**\n\nThe pyramids are among the most famous tourist sights in the world. Thousands of tourists flock to Egypt every year, specifically to this region. Even though it is widely cited as a tourist site in Cairo, it is in the Giza region, which is very close.\n\nPayment at the Giza complex is by card only; cash is no longer accepted. Buy tickets in advance online at egymonuments.com or book a guided tour.\n\n**Current entry fees (foreigners):**\n\n- Giza Plateau (general admission): **EGP 240 (~$12.50)**\n- Inside the Great Pyramid of Khufu: **EGP 700+ additional**\n- Inside Pyramid of Menkaure: **EGP 100 additional**\n\n![camel tour and selfie](https://wortheverymile.com/wp-content/uploads/2020/03/IMG_20210308_085752_141.jpg.webp)\n\nRead more on the **[necropolis and the pyramids](https://wortheverymile.com/en/africa/egypt/the-pyramids-of-giza/)**\n\n##### **Grand Egyptian Museum (GEM) — The World’s Largest Museum**\n\nSince its official opening in November 2023, the Grand Egyptian Museum has become the unmissable attraction in Cairo, and arguably all of Egypt. Located just 2km from the Giza Pyramids, the GEM is the largest museum in the world dedicated to a single civilization, housing over 100,000 artifacts, including the complete treasures of Tutankhamun displayed together for the first time in history.\n\n**Key facts:**\n\n- **Location:** Giza, next to the Ring Road — ~15 minutes from downtown Cairo\n- **Highlights:** Tutankhamun galleries (5,000+ artifacts), 11-meter statue of Ramses II, Solar Boat of Khufu, Grand Staircase with views of the Pyramids\n- **Tickets:** Foreign adults EGP 1,450 (~$29); students/children EGP 730. Must be booked online only at **[visit-gem.com](https://visit-gem.com/)**\n- **Hours:** 8:30 am–10:00 pm daily\n- **Tip:** Book tickets well in advance; the GEM sells out regularly. Also, browse **[GEM guided tours on GetYourGuide](https://gyg.me/F8TgIZBu)** for skip-the-line options with an Egyptologist.\n\n##### **Coptic Cairo**\n\nPart of Old Cairo, which witnessed the early stages of Christianity in Egypt, includes the Babylonian Fortress, the Coptic Museum, the Hanging Church, the Greek Orthodox Church of St. George, and several other Coptic Churches and historic sites. In Coptic Cairo, it is believed that the Holy Family visited the area and stayed at the Church of Saints Sergius and Bacchus (Abu Serga) to hide during Herod’s invasion. The cobbled alleys and fortress walls add a picturesque touch to your tour.\n\n![coptic cairo](https://wortheverymile.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/03/coptic-1.jpg.webp)\n\n##### **Cairo Citadel**\n\nOtherwise, the Acropolis of Cairo was built between 1176 and 1183 AD to prevent the Crusaders. It is known for its three mosques (Muhammed Ali, Al-Nasir, Muhammad, and Sulayman Pasha), which you will admire for their imposing Islamic architecture.\n\n![Cairo Citadel](https://wortheverymile.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/03/citadel-1-1.jpg.webp)\n\n##### **Amr Ibn Al-As Mosque**\n\nThis is the first mosque built in Egypt and throughout Africa. It is currently the fourth-largest mosque in the Islamic world. You will be asked, as in all mosques, to remove your shoes before entering, and women are given cloaks to wear over their clothes to cover their bodies.\n\n![amr ibn al as mosque at cairo](https://wortheverymile.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/03/IMG_20210306_171606.jpg.webp)\n\n##### **Al-Khalil Bazaar**\n\nIt is the largest, most famous, and oldest bazaar in Cairo. You can find everything from souvenirs, jewelry, and herbs to handmade clothes and rugs, literally. It was our choice to buy some souvenirs for our home and acquaintances, since we had heard there were great bargains on prices, something you should not be ashamed to do.\n\n![El khalil bazaar](https://wortheverymile.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/03/arnaud-mariat-jkcfb3RZxGE-unsplash-1.jpg.webp)\n\n##### **Walk with a felucca**\n\nA felucca was a type of small wooden boat used in the Eastern Mediterranean. The ideal time for your walk on the Nile with this floating vehicle is mainly in the afternoon, when the sun has set, making the landscape idyllic for unforgettable photos.\n\n##### **Cruise and dinner on the Nile**\n\nDelightful dinner along the Nile accompanied by music and oriental dances. The lights from the cruise ships and the feluccas that take a walk reflect on the Nile, creating a romantic atmosphere. There is usually a large buffet with a variety of local Egyptian dishes. The shows usually include belly dances by both men and women, along with other acts that even the guests can participate in. Based on our experience, the cruise lasts about 4 hours and costs 40 € per couple. Our hotelier had asked for double the commission. We recommend that you use an application, such as **[Getyourguide](https://www.getyourguide.com/cairo-l92/dinner-cruise-on-the-nile-maxiem-in-cairo-every-night-t11735/?partner_id=85CKKC8&utm_medium=affiliate&placement=button-cta)**. The reason is to avoid the commissions charged by travel agencies and hoteliers after first reading reviews of available tours.\n\n![Cairo night cruise](https://wortheverymile.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/03/IMG_20210314_200317-1.jpg.webp)\n\n##### **Al-Azhar Park**\n\nThe large park looks like an oasis of greenery in the city. Wander through its gardens and alleys, enjoy the fountains, and take a breath of fresh air away from the noise of the city. There is a mandatory entrance, and inside you will find cafes and restaurants. We preferred to eat at the Misr studio in the center of the park, where, in addition to the wonderful traditional food, we were impressed by the building’s architecture and the view of the Citadelle.\n\n##### **Excursion to Alexandria**\n\nThe city of **[Alexandria](https://wortheverymile.com/en/africa/egypt/travel-guide-for-alexandria/)** is less than 3 hours away. Alexandria is the second-largest city and the main port of Egypt. Known for its ancient lighthouse, library, and many more points of interest.\n\nBus routes from Cairo to Alexandria can be found on **[Bookaway](https://www.bookaway.com/?offer_id=34&aff_id=2419)**\n\n![Qaitbay castle in Alexandria](https://wortheverymile.com/wp-content/uploads/2020/07/castle-4581234_1920-1.jpg.webp)\n\n##### **Cairo Opera House**\n\nThe Cairo Opera House is a cultural landmark and performing arts center located in the heart of Cairo, Egypt. The opera house was inaugurated in 1988 and serves as the city’s main venue for the performing arts, hosting a wide range of events, including operas, ballets, concerts, and theatrical performances.\n\nThe opera house complex features several performance spaces, including the Main Hall, which seats up to 1,200 people and hosts large-scale productions such as operas and ballets. There is also the Small Hall, which seats up to 500 people and is used for smaller productions, such as chamber music concerts and theatrical performances.\n\nIn addition to the performance spaces, the Cairo Opera House also houses several other facilities, including rehearsal rooms, a music library, and an art gallery. The gallery displays a rotating collection of artwork from local and international artists. The architecture of the Cairo Opera House is stunning, with a modern design that incorporates traditional Egyptian motifs. The exterior of the building is adorned with sculptures and carvings that depict ancient Egyptian gods and symbols.\n\n##### **Cairo Tower**\n\nAt 187 meters, you will enjoy the panoramic view of the city. A tower whose shape symbolizes the flower of the Pharaohs, the Lotus. At the top, you will find the revolving platform with the restaurant, which offers a unique view of the pyramids, the Nile, and the whole city. Each rotation lasts 70 minutes.\n\n![Cairo Tower](https://wortheverymile.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/03/cairo-tower-1-1.jpg.webp)\n\n##### **Arabic coffee (or tea) and hookah**\n\nFans of coffee should try the Arabic coffee in a kettle with spices at a store you will find on your way. In these cafes (ahwa), they offer hookahs with special flavors such as apple smoke, and their local tea is just as wonderful. We recommend the Fishawy Coffee Café, the oldest coffee shop, named after the large mirrors and large wooden frames that decorate it.\n\n## Where to eat in Cairo\n\n**Sequoia**: Large armchairs and hanging lanterns offer views of the illuminated buildings along the Nile. Sequoia Restaurant will offer you high-quality Mediterranean cuisine.\n\n**Felfella:** In the city center, you will find our favorite Felfella restaurant, where you can taste traditional Egyptian food. The decoration with live quails and pigeons in cages (which we later saw on the list as stuffed or fried), the low lighting with oriental lanterns, and the service, in addition to their delicious food, won us over.\n\n**O ’Pasta**: For something different from the local flavors, O’ Pasta offers Italian cuisine with reasonable prices and amazing service.\n\n**Abou Tarek:** A good solution for fast, filling food in Cairo, Abou Tarek has served only Koshary since 1950.\n\nIn addition to traditional cuisine, you can eat at well-known international restaurant chains such as McDonald’s, KFC, and Pizza Hut.\n\n## When to go\n\nThe best time to visit Cairo is during the winter or early spring. During this period, the temperatures ​​are comfortable enough to move around the city without the hot sun spoiling your plans. We personally made our trip to Cairo in December. The weather was great, with temperatures up to 22 degrees at noon and a slight chill at night. However, the weather is not always stable; there are several changes during the day and large deviations from morning to night.\n\nThe summer months are almost forbidden for a trip to Cairo. The hot desert sun will keep you from enjoying your activities, and the high humidity is annoying. Rainfall is rare in winter and summer, so it’s hard for any rain to spoil your designs.\n\n## Useful Sites & Apps\n\nIn the last few years, we have been traveling and have singled out some applications we use on every trip. We have chosen them over others for their validity, ease of use, and the variety of options they offer. Below, we describe in detail what these are and their characteristics.\n\n##### **Booking.com**\n\nBooking.com is a popular online booking platform for accommodations worldwide. With a vast selection of hotels, apartments, villas, and more, Booking.com offers travelers the convenience of finding and booking their ideal stay in just a few clicks. The site’s user-friendly interface and extensive filtering options make it easy to narrow down search results based on specific preferences such as price, location, amenities, and more. As a result, Booking.com has become a go-to destination for travelers looking for hassle-free booking options. With competitive pricing and a loyalty program that rewards repeat bookings, Booking.com is a top choice for travelers seeking great deals and a seamless booking experience. For available rooms and prices, look at [**Booking.com**](https://www.booking.com/searchresults.el.html?ss=Cairo&label=gen173nr-10CAEoggI46AdIM1gEaFyIAQGYATO4ARfIAQzYAQPoAQH4AQGIAgGoAgG4Ava_qM4GwAIB0gIkNGE5N2RjYjYtYTMyMS00ZTJmLWFlNjEtZTExNTllZGRiYjU02AIB4AIB&sid=ba81ec935d2f2e2ac1b72dc29a634f78&aid=304142&lang=el&sb=1&src_elem=sb&src=index&dest_id=-290692&dest_type=city&ac_position=0&ac_click_type=b&ac_langcode=en&ac_suggestion_list_length=5&search_selected=true&search_pageview_id=27a35ce488080253&ac_meta=GhAyN2EzNWNlNDg4MDgwMjUzIAAoATICZW46BUNhaXJv&group_adults=2&no_rooms=1&group_children=0).\n\n##### **Welcome Pickups**\n\nOne of the best, most trustworthy, and most convenient 5-star airport taxi companies for your airport transfer based on user reviews across various platforms such as Google, Tripadvisor, and Trustpilot. Welcome Pickups offers a great experience and safety to its travelers. Local English-speaking drivers, flat, pre-paid fee, flight monitoring for delays, 24/7 Customer support + free cancellation. Check your options at **[Welcome Pickups](https://www.welcomepickups.com/?tap_a=26297-438366&tap_s=3089898-02f59c)**.\n\n##### **Air Bnb**\n\nWith more than 1 million properties worldwide, the options never end, ideal for low-budget opportunities even in expensive destinations. In addition, because it features homes and villas on its website, it helps you feel more familiar with each destination.\n\n##### **Hotels.com**\n\nHotels.com is a leading provider of hotel accommodation worldwide, offering booking services through its own network of localized websites. Once you reach 10 nights, you’ll have a free night, which you can apply at checkout. You can earn multiple free nights by continuing to book with Hotels.com, though. The platform gives travelers one of the widest selections of accommodation on the net, including both independent and major chain hotels as well as self-catering in over hundreds of thousands of properties worldwide. The company offers a one-stop shopping source for hotel pricing, amenities, and availability. Find the best deals on **[Hotels.com](https://www.hotels.com/affiliate?landingPage=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.hotels.com%2F%3Fpos%3DHCOM_US%26locale%3Den_US&camref=1101lu5WV&creativeref=1011l66481)**.\n\n##### **TripAdvisor**\n\nTripAdvisor helps hundreds of millions of people each month become better travelers, from planning to booking to taking a trip. Travelers across the globe use the Tripadvisor site and app to discover where to stay, what to do, and where to eat based on guidance from those who have been there before. With more than 1 billion reviews and opinions of nearly 8 million businesses, travelers turn to Tripadvisor to find deals on accommodations, book experiences, reserve tables at delicious restaurants, and discover great places nearby. Find available offers and book your accommodation on **[TripAdvisor](https://tripadvisor.tp.st/UlFAkxAI)**.\n\n##### **Omio.com**\n\nOmio is a leading travel platform that helps you easily plan and book your trips. With a comprehensive network of trains, buses, and flights across Europe and beyond, Omio allows you to compare and choose the best travel options for your needs. Whether you’re looking for a quick day trip or an extended vacation, Omio’s user-friendly interface and competitive prices make it easy to create unforgettable travel experiences. Check available routes on **[Omio.com](http://omio.sjv.io/PLkDY)**.\n\n##### **Skyscanner**\n\nSkyscanner is a leading global travel search engine, helping millions of travelers find the best flights, hotels, and car rentals. With a user-friendly interface and powerful search technology, Skyscanner makes it easy to compare prices and options across hundreds of travel providers, giving you the best deals for your trip. From budget to luxury, domestic to international, Skyscanner has everything you need to plan your next adventure. So whether you’re traveling for business or leisure, start your journey with Skyscanner and discover the world like never before. Available flights and prices can be found on **[Skyscanner](https://skyscanner.pxf.io/LX14xa)**.\n\n##### **GetYourGuide**\n\nGetYourGuide is an online marketplace for booking travel activities and experiences. With a wide variety of tours, activities, and attractions available in destinations around the world, GetYourGuide makes it easy to find and book unique experiences for your next trip. From city sightseeing tours and food and wine tastings to outdoor adventures and cultural experiences, GetYourGuide has something for every type of traveler. With a user-friendly website and mobile app, and a commitment to customer satisfaction, GetYourGuide is a top choice for travelers seeking memorable and authentic experiences. For available activities and tours, check on [**GetYourGuide**](https://www.getyourguide.com/cairo-l92/?partner_id=85CKKC8&utm_medium=affiliate&placement=button-cta&cmp=cairo).\n\n##### **Discover cars**\n\nDiscoverCars.com offers a hassle-free car rental booking experience, with a wide range of rental car options available worldwide. Whether you’re traveling for business or pleasure, DiscoverCars.com offers competitive rates, 24/7 customer support, and flexible cancellation policies. With no hidden fees and a user-friendly interface, booking your rental car has never been easier. Choose from a variety of car rental options, including economy, luxury, and family-friendly vehicles, and enjoy the freedom of the open road on your next adventure. Book with **[DiscoverCars.com](https://www.discovercars.com/?a_aid=stratisplakotaris)** today and start exploring the world on your own terms!\n\n##### **Hotel Tonight**\n\nHere you will find last-minute discounts on vacancies from hotels. This is an amazingly simple app, and it doesn’t take long to book a room. You may search in each town for sights and maps. Furthermore, you can check the reviews and photos of other travelers to be sure about the reliability of each hotel. It also has a 24/7 customer service. If you’re stuck somewhere and you need a room desperately, this is the perfect app for you.\n\n##### **City Maps 2Go**\n\nMaps for traveling, hiking, mountain cycling, and many other activities. A necessary app for every adventure, activity, or touring abroad. You may only download one map per country, whereas it was possible to download two in the past. For an additional map, you need to pay. Alternatively, as soon as you have looked at the information on a map, you can delete it and then download a new one.\n\nYou can mark all the places of interest on the map you have downloaded. Whether they are sights, shops, or restaurants, you can use the directions guide to get there. The great advantage of this app is that you don’t need to have an internet connection, although it might get stuck a few times. Nevertheless, it is quite a reliable and useful app.\n\n**EXTRA TIP**\n\nOnce you have marked all the places you want to visit before your trip, you can also book your accommodation accordingly. This way, you can be sure you’ll be staying somewhere close to all places of interest.\n\n##### **iVisa.com**\n\niVisa.com is an online platform that helps travelers obtain electronic visas, eVisas, and other travel documents quickly and easily. With an extensive database of travel requirements for over 200 countries and a user-friendly application process, iVisa.com makes it simple for travelers to apply for and obtain the necessary documents for their trips. Whether you’re planning a vacation, business trip, or other travel experience, iVisa.com can help you navigate the complexities of travel documentation with ease. More information for the necessary travel documents, depending on your nationality and destination, can be found on **[iVisa.com](https://www.ivisa.com/?utm_source=wortheverymile)**.\n\n## Cairo, Egypt FAQ — Everything you need to know\n\n**Where is Cairo, Egypt?**\n\nCairo is the capital and largest city of Egypt, located in northeast Africa near the Nile Delta. It sits approximately 160km south of the Mediterranean Sea. The Giza Pyramids, technically in the neighboring Giza Governorate, are within the Greater Cairo urban area.\n\n**What time is it in Cairo, Egypt?**\n\nCairo is in the Eastern European Time zone (EET) — UTC+2. Egypt does not observe daylight saving time, so the offset remains constant year-round. Cairo is 1 hour ahead of Central European Time (CET) and 7 hours ahead of US Eastern Standard Time.\n\n**What is the Grand Egyptian Museum?**\n\nThe Grand Egyptian Museum (GEM) is the world’s largest museum dedicated to a single civilization. Located next to the Giza Pyramids, it opened officially in November 2023 and houses over 100,000 artifacts, including the complete Tutankhamun collection. Tickets for foreigners cost EGP 1,450 (~$29) and must be booked online at visit-gem.com. Do not miss this, it is extraordinary.\n\n**Is Cairo safe to visit?**\n\nCairo is generally safe for tourists at the main attractions. The primary concerns are persistent touts around the Pyramids (read our **[Egypt tips guide](https://wortheverymile.com/en/africa/8-tips-for-your-first-trip-to-egypt/)**), chaotic traffic, and petty theft in crowds. Use Uber rather than street taxis, and stay in well-reviewed accommodation; you will have a wonderful time.\n\n[PrevPreviousAlexandria, Egypt: The ultimate travel guide for the city](https://wortheverymile.com/en/africa/egypt/travel-guide-for-alexandria/)\n\n[NextComplete guide to the Pyramids of To popular destinations, bus companies operate multiple trips per day, often round the clock at 30-60 minute intervals. To less traveled destinations, there may be only 1 scheduled trip a day. The most common bus types are non-air-conditioned, air-conditioned, and premium buses.\n\nIt is best to reserve your tickets at least a few days ahead of your travel date. Check our list of bus companies available for online booking offering Cairo to Hurghada tickets. When traveling on weekends and holidays it is recommended to reserve tickets much earlier due to higher numbers of travelers, both vacation-makers and urban workers returning home to their provincial home.\n\n## How far is it from Cairo to Hurghada?\n\nTraveling by bus, the distance from Cairo to Hurghada depends on the route taken. Most bus companies travel via expressways or tollways. This may not necessarily be the shortest route but it is the fastest one as normally there is less traffic and no traffic lights.\n\nTo get to certain island destinations which are not connected to the mainland by a bridge, buses board large car ferries. Passengers are usually asked to get off the bus, but there is no need to take your luggage from the bus. The travel distance from port to port is added to the total distance.\n\n## How long does it take to get from Cairo to Hurghada by Bus?\n\nThe estimate travel time from Cairo to Hurghada is about 6h 15m. Note that traveling by bus is slower compared to driving your own car or getting a taxi. Depending on the departure time congestion between Cairo and Hurghada may differ greatly There is usually more traffic between morning until afternoon during weekdays than during weekends.\n\nAnother important factor that may affect travel time is the type of bus. Premium or VIP buses are usually faster as they use motorways and call to fewer bus stations (if any) en route. Buses normally make short 10-30-minute breaks after a few hours of continuous travel to allow passengers use bathrooms, stock up on munchies and let bus attendants and the driver have rest.\n\nSome premium buses have onboard toilets and the second driver. Nevertheless, usually they still make en route stops for a quick lunch or fuel refill.\n\n## How much does it cost to get from Cairo to Hurghada by Bus?\n\nThe ticket price from Cairo to Hurghada can vary depending on the type of buses you choose. For a less expensive ticket consider non-air-conditioned or ordinary buses. Premium or luxury buses are more expensive but you can expect more comfort and some additional services like phone charging ports, personal entertainment system screens and toilets.\n\n## Cairo to Hurghada timetable\n\nTo help you plan your trip, here is the Cairo to Hurghada timetable of bus departure and arrival times.\n\n## Best Rated Companies for this Route\n\nBus companies differ a lot in quality and service they provide.         "url": "https://www.bootsnall.com/egypt/alexandria",
        "title": "Alexandria Travel Guide - BootsnAll",
        "description": "# Alexandria Travel Guide\n## BootsnAll Scorecard\n6.7 / 10\n\nAlexandria is a strong value, decent-food, low-crowd city whose two weak spots in 2026 are getting around, with the tram shut and the metro unfinished, and a security picture that is calm day to day but carries real caveats.\n\nValue8\n\nAt the 8 anchor, since Hostelworld dorms start at US$5.50, Qaitbay Citadel costs EGP 200 (about $3.80) and the Bibliotheca EGP 150, with the dollar-priced foreigner train fare from Cairo the one cost that stings.\n\nGetting around4.5\n\nBelow the 5 anchor in 2026, because the Raml tram has been fully suspended since April, the Abu Qir commuter line is closed for metro works, and taxis refuse meters, leaving Uber, Careem and haggling.\n\nCrowds and timing7\n\nCloser to 8 than 5, because foreign tour crowds are thin all year; July and August bring Cairo's summer exodus to the beaches, and December and January bring 2 to 2.4 inches of rain a month.\n\nSafety and hassle5.5\n\nNear the 5 anchor, with low everyday crime but two fatal attacks on foreigners in the city in 2023 and 2024, regular street harassment of women, unmetered taxis and a regional security picture the UK flags as unpredictable.\n\nFood and drink7.5\n\nGood and cheap if you eat like an Alexandrian, from fuul at Mohamed Ahmed to koshary topped with fried liver and grilled fish on the harbor, but the famous Greek-era dining rooms trade more on memory than cooking.\n\nIndie fit7.5\n\nRewards showing up alone, since there's no tour circuit to escape and the downtown cafés, bookstalls and sea walls are open to anyone, though the loss of the tram and the walled-off coast take some of the easy wandering away.\n\nEditorial scores, 1 to 10, against the same written anchors for every city. Half points allowed. The overall is the plain average of the six.\n\n## Getting There and Around\nMost travelers arrive overland from [Cairo](https://www.bootsnall.com/egypt/cairo), and the train is the way to do it. Since 2023 the best trains on the line have been Spanish-built Talgos, which leave Cairo's Ramses station at 08:00, 14:00 and 19:00 and reach Alexandria in two and a half hours, with VIP and plain express trains nearly as good on other departures. Foreigners pay dollar fares, in early 2026 $40 in second and $50 in first on the Talgo and $35 and $40 on the express trains, according to Seat61. The [Egyptian National Railways](https://enr.gov.eg/) app sells only Egyptian-price tickets, so buy at the Arab and Foreign reservation office at Ramses or through an agency. Your train will stop at Sidi Gaber, 3 km before the end of the line. Unless you're staying in the eastern districts, stay on to Misr Station downtown.\n\nAlexandria's airport is now called Alexandria International (ALY), formerly Borg El Arab (HBE), about 40 km southwest of the center. The name and codes changed on 4 September 2025, and a new terminal took over operations on 1 January 2025. It has direct flights from Istanbul on Turkish Airlines and Pegasus, Athens on Aegean and Air Cairo, Rome on Wizz Air, and a long list of Gulf cities. There is no public transport into town, and Wikivoyage puts the taxi ride at 60 to 90 minutes. For most long-haul trips it's simpler to fly into Cairo and take the train.\n\nEntry is the same as anywhere in Egypt. Your passport needs six months' validity. A Cabinet decision raises the single-entry visa to $33 from 1 January 2027.\n\nInside the city, plan on taxis and ride-hailing. Alexandria's black-and-yellow cabs don't use meters, so agree the price before you get in, or use Uber or Careem, both of which operate here. The Raml tram is suspended until the end of 2027, with replacement buses and minibuses running along Gamal Abdel Nasser Street and the Corniche every three to five minutes. Microbuses run the Corniche constantly and cost almost nothing, but drivers rarely speak English and routes change without the signs changing. Walking works well downtown, from Raml Station to Qaitbay is about 2.5 km along the water, but crossing the Corniche is a test of nerve, since drivers do not yield to pedestrians.\n\n## What's Changed Since 2016\nDecember 2022: Egypt began charging foreigners dollar prices for train tickets, and the Talgo trains arrived on the Cairo line soon after. The foreign fare on the Cairo line now runs $35 to $50 one way, and the Egyptian fare on the same train is far lower. It's still the best way to arrive, but it is no longer the bargain older guidebooks describe.\n\nFor most of the 2000s and 2010s, visitors couldn't see it at all. It's now the best single museum in the city, at EGP 400 for a foreign adult.\n\nJanuary to September 2025: the airport moved into its new Terminal 2 on 1 January, then dropped the Borg El Arab name on 4 September and took the code ALY, which used to belong to El Nouzha, the old city airport closed to traffic since January 2012.\n\nFebruary to April 2026: the Raml tram ran its last regular trips. A trial suspension between Victoria and Mostafa Kamel started on 1 February, partial closure on 11 February, and full suspension on 1 April. The rebuilt line, with 30 new Hyundai Rotem trams and track that rises above street level for part of the route, is meant to cut the end-to-end trip from 60 minutes to 35. The Abu Qir commuter railway is closed too, being converted into a 21.7 km metro line from Misr Station to Abu Qir.\n\nAugust 2026 onward: a digital visa on arrival started at Cairo airport on 1 August for $36, including a $6 service fee, and the base single-entry fee rises to $33 on 1 January 2027.\n\n## What the Usual Advice Gets Wrong\n**\"Fly into Borg El Arab (HBE), and don't confuse it with Alexandria's ALY airport.\"** That advice flipped in September 2025. Borg El Arab is now Alexandria International Airport, code ALY, and the old El Nouzha airport that used to carry that code has been closed to flights since 2012. Search fares under ALY.\n\n**\"Ride the historic double-decker tram along the coast for a few pounds.\"** The Raml line, the one with the double-deckers that ran parallel to the sea, has been fully suspended since 1 April 2026, with completion of its replacement contracted for the end of 2027. Replacement buses and minibuses cover the route. Reuters reported in January 2026 that the separate yellow City Line had not yet been slated for renovation.\n\n**\"The Cairo to Alexandria train is a cheap local ride, just buy a ticket on the app.\"** Foreigners have paid dollar fares since December 2022, $35 to $50 one way in early 2026, and the national railways app only sells Egyptian-price tickets. If cost matters more than comfort, intercity buses run from Cairo for roughly EGP 100 to 300 and shared minibuses from beside Ramses Station for about EGP 140.\n\n**\"Visit the site of the ancient Library and the Pharos Lighthouse.\"** Nothing of either survives above ground.\n\n**\"Hail a taxi and ask for the meter.\"** Alexandria's taxis refuse to use meters because the rates haven't been updated in years. Agree a price before you get in, or use Uber or Careem, which saves the argument.\n\n## Realities to Be Aware Of\nAt about EGP 52.3 to the dollar in 2026, a budget traveler can manage on $25 to $35 a day with a dorm bed from $5.50 to $12, fuul, koshary and microbuses, and one paid site. A mid-range day of $70 to $120 buys a private room, taxis or Uber, sit-down fish dinners and two or three sites. Comfortable travel at $200 and up means the Cecil or the Four Seasons. Entry fees are small, EGP 200 to 400 for the main sites, but the train from Cairo at $35 to $50 each way is often the single biggest cost.\n\nSafety needs a real paragraph. In October 2023 a policeman shot dead two Israeli tourists and their Egyptian guide at Pompey's Pillar, and the UK Foreign Office lists the killing of an Israeli-Canadian in the city in 2024. Neither the UK nor Canada advises against travel to Alexandria, but read your government's advice before you go and check it again on the way.\n\nAlexandria is conservative. Ignoring it is the usual advice.\n\nCarry cash in small notes. You can bring in at most EGP 5,000, and many shops only take cash or add a fee for cards. Summer is the crowded season. From late June to August the city fills with Egyptians escaping Cairo's heat, and the beaches are packed.\n\n## If Alexandria Is Part of a Longer Trip\nAlexandria fits as the Mediterranean end of an Egypt loop rather than a stop of its own. Cairo is two and a half hours away by Talgo, and Luxor and Aswan are reached by changing trains in Cairo, where the night sleepers leave. A common sequence is to fly into Cairo, give Alexandria three nights, come back to Cairo, then head south. If you're adding the Red Sea, [Sharm el-Sheikh](https://www.bootsnall.com/egypt/sharm-el-sheikh) has seasonal flights from Alexandria on Air Cairo and Egyptair. Coming the other way in 2027, the high-speed line is due to link Cairo and the coast at up to 250 km per hour, though its Alexandria stations are planned for the city's outskirts.\n\nWest along the coast, El Alamein is an easy day and Marsa Matruh is further on, reached by slow ordinary trains from Moharam Bek, plus an air-conditioned express from June to September. Don't plan to cross into Libya. The border area is off limits, and the UK advises against all travel within 20 km of it.\n\nThat makes it a useful open-jaw point. Arrive in Cairo, leave from Alexandria for Athens or Istanbul, and skip the backtrack. If you're stringing several countries together, [AirTreks](https://www.airtreks.com/) can build a multi-stop ticket around that kind of routing.\n\n## Sources\n-         "markdown": "![](https://cdn.sanity.io/images/0amkju8i/production/a93201e0e3ffd972d02f283384aa11c86aced0c3-3020x2264.jpg?rect=0,264,3020,1737&w=40&h=23&blur=30&q=20&auto=format)![The Corniche curving along the Mediterranean in Alexandria, Egypt, beside palm-lined Saad Zaghloul Square](https://cdn.sanity.io/images/0amkju8i/production/a93201e0e3ffd972d02f283384aa11c86aced0c3-3020x2264.jpg?rect=0,283,3020,1699&w=1920&h=1080&q=75&auto=format)\n\n# Alexandria Travel Guide\n\nAlexandria in 2026 is a city between transit systems, with the Raml tram shut for a rebuild, the metro and high-speed rail promised for 2027, and the Corniche wider and more walled off from the sea. Underneath that, Egypt's old Mediterranean port is still cheap, slow and very much itself.\n\nPhoto: [Francisco Anzola](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Alexandria_Corniche_(2346973947).jpg), [CC BY 2.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/2.0), via Wikimedia Commons\n\nUpdated October 4, 2026 · On BootsnAll since 2006 · [History](https://www.bootsnall.com/egypt/alexandria#update-history)\n\n## BootsnAll Scorecard\n\n6.7 / 10\n\nAlexandria is a strong value, decent-food, low-crowd city whose two weak spots in 2026 are getting around, with the tram shut and the metro unfinished, and a security picture that is calm day to day but carries real caveats.\n\nValue8\n\nAt the 8 anchor, since Hostelworld dorms start at US$5.50, Qaitbay Citadel costs EGP 200 (about $3.80) and the Bibliotheca EGP 150, with the dollar-priced foreigner train fare from Cairo the one cost that stings.\n\nGetting around4.5\n\nBelow the 5 anchor in 2026, because the Raml tram has been fully suspended since April, the Abu Qir commuter line is closed for metro works, and taxis refuse meters, leaving Uber, Careem and haggling.\n\nCrowds and timing7\n\nCloser to 8 than 5, because foreign tour crowds are thin all year; July and August bring Cairo's summer exodus to the beaches, and December and January bring 2 to 2.4 inches of rain a month.\n\nSafety and hassle5.5\n\nNear the 5 anchor, with low everyday crime but two fatal attacks on foreigners in the city in 2023 and 2024, regular street harassment of women, unmetered taxis and a regional security picture the UK flags as unpredictable.\n\nFood and drink7.5\n\nGood and cheap if you eat like an Alexandrian, from fuul at Mohamed Ahmed to koshary topped with fried liver and grilled fish on the harbor, but the famous Greek-era dining rooms trade more on memory than cooking.\n\nIndie fit7.5\n\nRewards showing up alone, since there's no tour circuit to escape and the downtown cafés, bookstalls and sea walls are open to anyone, though the loss of the tram and the walled-off coast take some of the easy wandering away.\n\nEditorial scores, 1 to 10, against the same written anchors for every city. Half points allowed. The overall is the plain average of the six.\n\n## Overview and Things to Consider\n\nAlexandria is Egypt's second city, about 5.4 million people strung along the Mediterranean for some 40 km, and it doesn't look or feel like Cairo. The light is softer, the air is wet, the buildings downtown are Italianate apartment blocks with peeling shutters, and the city's spine is a single seafront road, the Corniche, that runs from the Qaitbay Citadel in the west to Montaza in the east. Nearly everything a visitor wants sits in the first 5 or 6 km of it.\n\nYou'll like Alexandria if you enjoy cities more than monuments: old cafés, secondhand bookstalls, fish markets, long sea walks, the residue of a cosmopolitan past. You'll be disappointed if you came for pharaonic grandeur. The ancient city that made the name, the Library and the Lighthouse, is gone or underwater, and what's left from antiquity is mostly Roman and underground. If you've just done Luxor, the catacombs here will feel small.\n\nThe 2026 moment is construction. The blue-and-cream Raml tram, running since 1863, was fully suspended on 1 April 2026 for a rebuild contracted to finish by the end of 2027. The Abu Qir commuter line is being turned into a metro. Egypt's first high-speed line, Ain Sokhna to Marsa Matrouh via Alexandria's outskirts, is now targeted for July 2027. For a visitor this year, that means more taxis, more traffic and fewer of the slow rides that used to be the easiest way to see the city.\n\nThe beaches in town are overrated, crowded and littered in summer and shrinking as the road widens. What's underrated is a morning in the downtown grid around Raml Station, eating breakfast at a fuul counter and walking it off along Nabi Daniel Street.\n\n## How Alexandria Got Here\n\nAlexander the Great founded the city in 331 BCE on a fishing village called Rhakotis, and under the Ptolemies it became the capital of Egypt and the intellectual center of the Mediterranean. They built the Pharos lighthouse, just over 100 m tall, one of the Seven Wonders, and a library that tried to collect every text in the known world. Earthquakes in 956, 1303 and 1323 wrecked the lighthouse, and in 1477 Sultan Qaitbay reused its stone for the fort that stands on the spot today. After the Arab conquest in 641, the capital moved inland to what became Cairo, and Alexandria slid into a long decline as a trading port.\n\nThe modern city is a 19th-century invention. Muhammad Ali reconnected it to the Nile with the Mahmoudiyah Canal in 1820, Greeks, Italians, Jews, Armenians and Levantines poured in, and the Raml tram and the Corniche followed. British warships bombarded the center in 1882. The cosmopolitan city of Cavafy, Forster and Durrell ended in the 1950s, when Nasser's nationalizations after the 1956 Suez Crisis pushed out nearly all the foreign communities, including a Greek population once around 150,000. What you see downtown, the Greek café names, the French street signs, the synagogue on Nabi Daniel Street, is what they left behind.\n\n## Getting There and Around\n\nMost travelers arrive overland from [Cairo](https://www.bootsnall.com/egypt/cairo), and the train is the way to do it. Since 2023 the best trains on the line have been Spanish-built Talgos, which leave Cairo's Ramses station at 08:00, 14:00 and 19:00 and reach Alexandria in two and a half hours, with VIP and plain express trains nearly as good on other departures. Foreigners pay dollar fares, in early 2026 $40 in second and $50 in first on the Talgo and $35 and $40 on the express trains, according to Seat61. The [Egyptian National Railways](https://enr.gov.eg/) app sells only Egyptian-price tickets, so buy at the Arab and Foreign reservation office at Ramses or through an agency. Your train will stop at Sidi Gaber, 3 km before the end of the line. Unless you're staying in the eastern districts, stay on to Misr Station downtown.\n\nAlexandria's airport is now called Alexandria International (ALY), formerly Borg El Arab (HBE), about 40 km southwest of the center. The name and codes changed on 4 September 2025, and a new terminal took over operations on 1 January 2025. It has direct flights from Istanbul on Turkish Airlines and Pegasus, Athens on Aegean and Air Cairo, Rome on Wizz Air, and a long list of Gulf cities. There is no public transport into town, and Wikivoyage puts the taxi ride at 60 to 90 minutes. For most long-haul trips it's simpler to fly into Cairo and take the train.\n\nEntry is the same as anywhere in Egypt. US, UK, EU, Canadian and Australian passport holders need a visa, either an e-visa from the official [visa2egypt portal](https://www.visa2egypt.gov.eg/), which asks you to apply at least seven days ahead, or a 30-day visa on arrival at airport kiosks for $30, cash only, according to the [UK Foreign Office](https://www.gov.uk/foreign-travel-advice/egypt). Your passport needs six months' validity. A Cabinet decision raises the single-entry visa to $33 from 1 January 2027.\n\nInside the city, plan on taxis and ride-hailing. Alexandria's black-and-yellow cabs don't use meters, so agree the price before you get in, or use Uber or Careem, both of which operate here. The Raml tram is suspended until the end of 2027, with replacement buses and minibuses running along Gamal Abdel Nasser Street and the Corniche every three to five minutes. Microbuses run the Corniche constantly and cost almost nothing, but drivers rarely speak English and routes change without the signs changing. Walking works well downtown, from Raml Station to Qaitbay is about 2.5 km along the water, but crossing the Corniche is a test of nerve, since drivers do not yield to pedestrians.\n\n## What's Changed Since 2016\n\nDecember 2022: Egypt began charging foreigners dollar prices for train tickets, and the Talgo trains arrived on the Cairo line soon after. The foreign fare on the Cairo line now runs $35 to $50 one way, and the Egyptian fare on the same train is far lower. It's still the best way to arrive, but it is no longer the bargain older guidebooks describe.\n\nOctober 2023: the Graeco-Roman Museum reopened after being closed since 2005, with about 10,000 objects in 25 galleries covering the city from Alexander to the Arab conquest. For most of the 2000s and 2010s, visitors couldn't see it at all. It's now the best single museum in the city, at EGP 400 for a foreign adult.\n\nJanuary to September 2025: the airport moved into its new Terminal 2 on 1 January, then dropped the Borg El Arab name on 4 September and took the code ALY, which used to belong to El Nouzha, the old city airport closed to traffic since January 2012.\n\nFebruary to April 2026: the Raml tram ran its last regular trips. A trial suspension between Victoria and Mostafa Kamel started on 1 February, partial closure on 11 February, and full suspension on 1 April. The rebuilt line, with 30 new Hyundai Rotem trams and track that rises above street level for part of the route, is meant to cut the end-to-end trip from 60 minutes to 35. The Abu Qir commuter railway is closed too, being converted into a 21.7 km metro line from Misr Station to Abu Qir.\n\nAugust 2026 onward: a digital visa on arrival started at Cairo airport on 1 August for $36, including a $6 service fee, and the base single-entry fee rises to $33 on 1 January 2027. The UK Foreign Office's Egypt advice, updated 4 August 2026, also updated its section on regional tensions, noting strikes across the region against US targets from 8 July.\n\n## What the Usual Advice Gets Wrong\n\n**\"Fly into Borg El Arab (HBE), and don't confuse it with Alexandria's ALY airport.\"** That advice flipped in September 2025. Borg El Arab is now Alexandria International Airport, code ALY, and the old El Nouzha airport that used to carry that code has been closed to flights since 2012. Search fares under ALY.\n\n**\"Ride the historic double-decker tram along the coast for a few pounds.\"** The Raml line, the one with the double-deckers that ran parallel to the sea, has been fully suspended since 1 April 2026, with completion of its replacement contracted for the end of 2027. Replacement buses and minibuses cover the route. Reuters reported in January 2026 that the separate yellow City Line had not yet been slated for renovation.\n\n**\"The Cairo to Alexandria train is a cheap local ride, just buy a ticket on the app.\"** Foreigners have paid dollar fares since December 2022, $35 to $50 one way in early 2026, and the national railways app only sells Egyptian-price tickets. If cost matters more than comfort, intercity buses run from Cairo for roughly EGP 100 to 300 and shared minibuses from beside Ramses Station for about EGP 140.\n\n**\"Visit the site of the ancient Library and the Pharos Lighthouse.\"** Nothing of either survives above ground. The Bibliotheca Alexandrina is a 2002 building near the ancient library's presumed location, and Qaitbay Citadel was built from the lighthouse's fallen stone. The lighthouse's remains lie on the seabed, and the closest you'll get on land is the underwater finds in the National Museum and the Bibliotheca's Antiquities Museum.\n\n**\"Hail a taxi and ask for the meter.\"** Alexandria's taxis refuse to use meters because the rates haven't been updated in years. Agree a price before you get in, or use Uber or Careem, which saves the argument. Canada's advisory also tells women not to sit in the front seat.\n\n## Ideas to Consider for Your Visit\n\nHave you thought about spending real time inside the [Bibliotheca Alexandrina](https://www.bibalex.org/) instead of photographing it from the Corniche? The main reading room drops down eleven terraced levels under a 32 m tilted glass roof, and the outer granite wall is carved with characters from 120 scripts. A foreigner's ticket is EGP 150, or EGP 300 for the inclusive ticket that adds the Antiquities Museum and the Manuscripts Museum. English guided tours run several times a day Sunday to Thursday, and the Antiquities Museum is where you'll see the objects raised from the harbor floor.\n\nDo the western end on foot. Start at [Qaitbay Citadel](https://egymonuments.gov.eg/en/monuments/qaitbay-fort), built from 1477 to 1479 on the lighthouse site, open 9am to 8pm and EGP 200 for foreigners. Climb the keep for the view back across the Eastern Harbor, then walk back through Anfushi and Bahary past the fishing boats. Stop at the Abu al-Abbas al-Mursi Mosque, built over the tomb of a 13th-century Sufi saint, and get Alexandrian ice cream, slightly stretchy, at one of the Bahary shops such as Azza or El Sheikh Wafik.\n\nGive the Roman city a half day. The [Graeco-Roman Museum](https://egymonuments.gov.eg/en/museums/graeco-roman-museum) reopened in 2023 and is open 9am to 5pm Sunday to Thursday and until 8pm on Friday and Saturday. Kom el-Dikka, just north of Misr Station, has Egypt's only Roman amphitheater, 13 marble tiers seating about 800, and the mosaics of the Villa of the Birds. South in Karmouz, the Catacombs of Kom el-Shoqafa were found in 1900 when a donkey fell through the ground, and their carved tombs blend Egyptian gods with Roman dress. Pompey's Pillar, a 25 m granite column from 297 CE, is nearby.\n\nEat the way the city does. Breakfast is fuul and falafel at Mohamed Ahmed on Shakour Street, off Saad Zaghloul Street. Lunch can be koshary topped with kibda Iskandarani, liver fried with cumin, onion and hot peppers. For grilled fish, try places like [Shaaban](https://www.shabanseafood.com/en/home) out in El Max or the White and Blue terrace at the Greek Club beside Qaitbay. Have om ali at Trianon, the café that has been on Saad Zaghloul Square since 1905.\n\nRide out to El Alamein, 120 km west, where the 1942 battles turned the North African war. Trains for the coast leave from Moharam Bek station, 4 km south of the main station, not from Misr Station, and there are hourly buses too. The Commonwealth War Cemetery holds 7,367 graves, the military museum is 300 m west of its gate, and the German and Italian memorials are about 10 km further on. It makes a long day out.\n\nRosetta, 65 km east where a branch of the Nile meets the sea, is where the Rosetta Stone turned up in 1799, at Fort Julien, then held by Napoleon's army. The stone itself is in the British Museum, but the town keeps a center of Ottoman merchant houses, and few other visitors. Pair it with Abu Qir, 23 km northeast of Alexandria, where Nelson fought the Battle of the Nile in 1798, beside the ruins of ancient Canopus.\n\n### The Part Most Guides Miss\n\nNabi Daniel Street, the pedestrian market street that runs from Misr Station up toward the Corniche, is the best walk in the city and almost no guide sells it as one. It's lined with booksellers with titles in several languages, and further inland stalls sell plug adapters and electrical parts. At number 69 is the Eliyahu Hanavi Synagogue, completed in 1850 and rededicated in January 2020 after a government restoration, with three Jews at the ceremony. Number 30 is the [Institut Français](https://www.ifegypte.com/alexandrie/), whose courtyard café is one of the quietest places downtown to sit. Walk it slowly from the station north toward the sea, and you'll pass most of what's left of cosmopolitan Alexandria in about 15 minutes.\n\n## Where to Base Yourself\n\n**Raml Station and Mansheya.** The old downtown around Saad Zaghloul Square is where to stay on a short trip. You can walk to the Bibliotheca, Qaitbay, the museums and Misr Station, and the budget hotels on El Ghorfa El Togaria Street and the Ithaka hostels, dorms from about $12 on Hostelworld, are here. So are the grand old hotels, the [Steigenberger Cecil](https://www.steigenberger.com/en/hotels/all-hotels/egypt/alexandria/steigenberger-cecil-hotel) and Le Metropole. The catch is noise, from Corniche traffic and wedding parties, and buildings that are atmospheric in the brochure sense and tired in person.\n\n**Stanley, Roushdy and San Stefano.** The eastern seafront districts are where middle-class Alexandria lives, eats and shops, around Stanley Bridge, the cafés of Roushdy and the San Stefano Grand Plaza mall with the Four Seasons. They suit longer stays and people who'd rather eat out than sightsee. The catch in 2026 is distance. These neighborhoods were tied to downtown by the Raml tram, and without it you're relying on taxis and replacement buses for a ride of 5 to 8 km in heavy traffic.\n\n**Montaza.** At the far eastern end, the Montaza palace grounds give you gardens, sea and the Helnan Palestine and Salamlek hotels, and in summer it's where Egyptian families come for the beach. It suits a couple of days of quiet, not sightseeing, since downtown is about 15 km away. Also, Rixos signed a deal in 2024 to develop the palace itself into a hotel, so the palace itself may be under works.\n\n## Realities to Be Aware Of\n\nAt about EGP 52.3 to the dollar in 2026, a budget traveler can manage on $25 to $35 a day with a dorm bed from $5.50 to $12, fuul, koshary and microbuses, and one paid site. A mid-range day of $70 to $120 buys a private room, taxis or Uber, sit-down fish dinners and two or three sites. Comfortable travel at $200 and up means the Cecil or the Four Seasons. Entry fees are small, EGP 200 to 400 for the main sites, but the train from Cairo at $35 to $50 each way is often the single biggest cost.\n\nSafety needs a real paragraph. Everyday crime is low, but foreigners have been targeted here. In October 2023 a policeman shot dead two Israeli tourists and their Egyptian guide at Pompey's Pillar, and the UK Foreign Office lists the killing of an Israeli-Canadian in the city in 2024. In August 2026 the UK updated its section on regional tensions, noting strikes against US targets across the region since 8 July. Neither the UK nor Canada advises against travel to Alexandria, but read your government's advice before you go and check it again on the way.\n\nAlexandria is conservative. Women should cover shoulders, midriff and legs, and should expect staring, comments and sometimes groping, especially walking alone and on public holidays. Ignoring it is the usual advice. Canada advises men not to wear shorts outside tourist areas. Same-sex relationships aren't explicitly criminalized, but Canada warns that police use dating apps to set up arrests, so LGBTQ+ travelers should be very discreet. Don't photograph police, military sites or government buildings, don't bring a drone, which needs prior Defence Ministry permission, and don't post political criticism of Egypt online.\n\nCarry cash in small notes. You can bring in at most EGP 5,000, and many shops only take cash or add a fee for cards. Cover your head entering mosques, and note that the coolest months are also the wettest. Summer is the crowded season. From late June to August the city fills with Egyptians escaping Cairo's heat, and the beaches are packed.\n\n### What things cost in Alexandria\n\nPrices checked October 2026. Each links to where we read it.\n\n| Item | Price | Source |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Hostel dorm bed | US$5.50 to about $12 a night | [Hostelworld, Oct 2026](https://www.hostelworld.com/st/hostels/africa/egypt/alexandria/) |\n| Private hostel room | From US$7.50, about $22 at Ithaka El Raml | [Hostelworld, Oct 2026](https://www.hostelworld.com/st/hostels/africa/egypt/alexandria/) |\n| Cairo to Alexandria Talgo train, foreigner fare | $40 second class, $50 first class, one way | [Seat61, early 2026 fares](https://www.seat61.com/Egypt.htm) |\n| Cairo to Alexandria shared minibus | About EGP 140 (under $3) | [Wikivoyage, Oct 2025](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Alexandria) |\n| Qaitbay Citadel, foreign adult | EGP 200 (about $3.80); students EGP 100 | [Ministry of Tourism and Antiquities](https://egymonuments.gov.eg/en/monuments/qaitbay-fort) |\n| Graeco-Roman Museum, foreign adult | EGP 400 (about $7.65); students EGP 200 | [Ministry of Tourism and Antiquities](https://egymonuments.gov.eg/en/museums/graeco-roman-museum) |\n| Bibliotheca Alexandrina, non-Egyptian adult | EGP 150 main library (about $2.90); EGP 300 inclusive ticket | [Bibliotheca Alexandrina](https://www.bibalex.org/en/Page/BA_Visiting) |\n| Visa on arrival, single entry | $30 cash; $36 digital at Cairo airport; $33 base fee from 1 Jan 2027 | [UK FCDO and VisasNews, Aug-Sep 2026](https://www.gov.uk/foreign-travel-advice/egypt/entry-requirements) |\n\nDollar figures at the Central Bank of Egypt's rate of about EGP 52.3 to US$1 on 1 October 2026.\n\n## If Alexandria Is Part of a Longer Trip\n\nAlexandria fits as the Mediterranean end of an Egypt loop rather than a stop of its own. Cairo is two and a half hours away by Talgo, and Luxor and Aswan are reached by changing trains in Cairo, where the night sleepers leave. A common sequence is to fly into Cairo, give Alexandria three nights, come back to Cairo, then head south. If you're adding the Red Sea, [Sharm el-Sheikh](https://www.bootsnall.com/egypt/sharm-el-sheikh) has seasonal flights from Alexandria on Air Cairo and Egyptair. Coming the other way in 2027, the high-speed line is due to link Cairo and the coast at up to 250 km per hour, though its Alexandria stations are planned for the city's outskirts.\n\nWest along the coast, El Alamein is an easy day and Marsa Matruh is further on, reached by slow ordinary trains from Moharam Bek, plus an air-conditioned express from June to September. From Matruh it's 300 km of desert road to Siwa oasis, but the UK advises against all but essential travel to the Western Desert outside a few listed areas, and Siwa is not one of them, while Canada says to use only the Matruh road. Don't plan to cross into Libya. The border area is off limits, and the UK advises against all travel within 20 km of it.\n\nFor onward flights, Alexandria has direct connections to [Athens](https://www.bootsnall.com/greece/athens) on Aegean and Air Cairo, [Istanbul](https://www.bootsnall.com/turkey/istanbul) on Turkish and Pegasus, [Amman](https://www.bootsnall.com/jordan/amman) on Air Arabia and Air Cairo, and Gulf hubs including [Doha](https://www.bootsnall.com/qatar/doha) and [Dubai](https://www.bootsnall.com/uae/dubai). That makes it a useful open-jaw point. Arrive in Cairo, leave from Alexandria for Athens or Istanbul, and skip the backtrack. If you're stringing several countries together, [AirTreks](https://www.airtreks.com/) can build a multi-stop ticket around that kind of routing.\n\n## Yearly Things to Consider\n\nThe sea drives everything here. Summer, late June through August, is high season for Egyptians, with highs around 88°F (31°C), heavy humidity, and packed beaches. For a foreign visitor, the best months are April to early June and late September to November, warm, dry and quiet. Winter is mild at 65°F (18°C) in January, but it is one of Egypt's two wettest places, and a December or January visit can mean storms, flooded streets and grey seas.\n\nCounterintuitively, the months Egyptians pick for Alexandria are the ones a foreign visitor should avoid. July and August are its peak, warm, humid and crowded with families from Cairo, while April, May and October are quiet, dry and still warm enough to sit out on the Corniche at night. Plan around Ramadan too. Canada expects the 2027 fast to begin around 7 February, which shortens site hours and moves the city's eating to after sunset.\n\n### Alexandria month by month\n\n| Month | Temp | Rainfall | Season | Notes |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| January | 65°F (18°C) | 2.4 in | Low | The wettest month. Storms and flooded streets possible. |\n| February | 66°F (19°C) | 1.4 in | Low | Cool and showery. Ramadan expected from about 7 Feb in 2027. |\n| March | 70°F (21°C) | 0.5 in | Shoulder | Rain tails off. Good walking weather. |\n| April | 75°F (24°C) | 0.1 in | Shoulder | Among the best months. Dry, mild, quiet. |\n| May | 80°F (27°C) | 0.04 in | Shoulder | Warm and dry. Sea still cool for swimming. |\n| June | 84°F (29°C) | 0 in | Shoulder | Humidity climbs. Domestic crowds arrive late in the month. |\n| July | 87°F (31°C) | 0 in | High | Cairo empties into Alexandria. Beaches packed. |\n| August | 88°F (31°C) | 0 in | High | Hottest month. Crowded Corniche and beaches. |\n| September | 86°F (30°C) | 0.03 in | Shoulder | Sea at its warmest. Crowds thin after school starts. |\n| October | 82°F (28°C) | 0.3 in | Shoulder | A strong month. Warm sea, first rain late. |\n| November | 75°F (24°C) | 1.4 in | Low | Rain returns. Mild days, few visitors. |\n| December | 68°F (20°C) | 2.1 in | Low | Wet and windy spells. Few foreign visitors. |\n\n## Ideas for Itineraries\n\n### 3 Days in Alexandria\n\nDay one, downtown and the Romans: fuul at Mohamed Ahmed, the Graeco-Roman Museum when it opens at 9, Kom el-Dikka's amphitheater, then a taxi to the Kom el-Shoqafa catacombs and Pompey's Pillar. End with coffee at Brazilian Coffee Stores on Saad Zaghloul Street and om ali at Trianon. Day two, the waterfront: the Bibliotheca in the morning with an English tour, then the walk along the Eastern Harbor to Qaitbay for late afternoon light, back through Anfushi for grilled fish and Bahary ice cream. Day three, Nabi Daniel Street, the National Museum on Tariq el-Horreyya, and an evening drink at the Cap d'Or, one of the city's oldest bars.\n\n### 5 Days in Alexandria\n\nTake the three days above at a slower pace, then use day four for El Alamein by train from Moharam Bek station, with the Commonwealth cemetery, the military museum and the German and Italian memorials, or for Rosetta and Abu Qir to the east by hired car. On day five, go east to Stanley and Roushdy for lunch with the locals, walk the seafront by Stanley Bridge, and finish in the Montaza gardens before sunset.\n\n### 1 Week in Alexandria\n\nA week lets you build Alexandria into a loop. Arrive by Talgo from Cairo, spend four nights downtown doing the city at walking pace, with one whole day for the Bibliotheca, its Antiquities and Manuscripts Museums and the planetarium. Then add two days on the coast, a night in El Alamein or Marsa Matruh, before heading back to Cairo. A week also leaves room for an evening at the Institut Français and a second, slower morning in the Graeco-Roman Museum.\n\n### 2 Weeks or More in Alexandria\n\nFor a longer stay, rent an apartment by the week in Roushdy, Stanley or the streets just inland from Raml Station, and look at it in the late afternoon before you pay, to check the light and the roaches. Alexandria is one of the better places in Egypt to study Arabic, with one-to-one teaching at Ahlan Egypt near Mansheya Square and the TAFL Center at Alexandria University, and French, German and Spanish cultural institutes for evenings out. Weekends work for Cairo by train, El Alamein and the coast, or Rosetta, and the Bibliotheca's reading room is a good place to spend weekdays.\n\n## Sources\n\nWhat this guide is built on. Alexandria](https://www.hostelworld.com/st/hostels/africa/egypt/alexandria/), Hostelworld (accessed Oct 3, 2026)\n\n## Alexandria Travel FAQ\n\nIs Alexandria worth an overnight, or is a day trip from Cairo enough?\n\nStay at least two nights. A day trip means five hours on trains and a rushed loop of the Bibliotheca and Qaitbay, and you'll miss the evenings, which is when the Corniche and the downtown cafés come alive. With the tram shut, getting between sites takes longer in 2026 than it used to.\n\nCan you drink alcohol in Alexandria?\n\nYes, though less openly than in the city's past. Drinkie's, a liquor store chain, has about a dozen branches, including one on Saad Zaghloul Square open from noon to 2am. Free-standing bars are few, the best known being the Cap d'Or and the Spitfire downtown, and most drinking happens in hotels.\n\nDo I need cash, and in what currency?\n\nMostly Egyptian pounds in small notes for food, taxis and tips. Bring US dollars in cash for the $30 visa on arrival, which is cash only, and know that Egypt limits what you can carry in or out to EGP 5,000. Cards work in hotels, malls and larger shops, but many small businesses take only cash or add a fee.\n\nCan I swim in the sea in Alexandria?\n\nYou can, but the city beaches are a poor version of it. Most public beaches charge for entry in summer, are packed with umbrellas and plastic chairs, and have rubbish in the water. Hotels such as the Four Seasons have private beaches, and Marina El Alamein, the upmarket resort about 100 km west, is the beach Egyptians with money drive to.\n\nCan I bring a drone or film the city?\n\nLeave the drone at home. Drones need prior Defence Ministry permission in Egypt, and unauthorized use can bring charges similar to espionage. Professional video gear, large cameras and even binoculars may be held at customs without prior approval, and photographing bridges, police, military sites or government buildings is prohibited. Phone photos of the sights are fine, and the Bibliotheca's Antiquities Museum lets you shoot with a phone for free.\n\nHow do I get to the El Alamein war cemeteries without a tour?\n\nTake a train or bus west. Coast trains leave from Moharam Bek station, about 4 km south of the main station, and the morning departures leave enough time to see the Commonwealth cemetery, which is staffed Saturday to Thursday from 7:30am to 2:30pm, and the military museum before heading back. Taxi drivers at Moharam Bek may tell you there are no trains in order to sell you a ride. Ignore them.\n\nUpdate history (9)▸▾\n\n1. October 3, 2026 · Published\n\nNew guide, researched from current sources and fact-checked before publishing\n\n3. 2012 · Update\n\nAnnual update by Adam Seper, including traveler dispatches and AirTreks returnees\n\n4. 2011 · Contributors\n\n1 member blog post from 1 blogger\n\n   - 2011: 1 post from 1 traveler on BootsnAll Travel Blogs ( [source](https://blogs.bootsnall.com/))\n5. May 16, 2008 · Archive\n\nTravel guide on BootsnAll, at a new address\n    [Archived copy of /Africa-Egypt-Alexandria](https://web.archive.org/web/20080516005617/http://www.bootsnall.com/Africa-Egypt-Alexandria)\n6. 2008 · Contributors\n\n7 member blog posts from 5 bloggers\n\n   - 2008: 7 posts from 5 travelers on BootsnAll Travel Blogs ( [source](https://blogs.bootsnall.com/))\n7. 2007 · Contributors\n\n3 member blog posts from 3 bloggers\n\n   - 2007: 3 posts from 3 travelers on BootsnAll Travel Blogs ( [source](https://blogs.bootsnall.com/))\n8. 2006 · Contributors\n\n2 member blog posts from 1 blogger\n\n   - 2006: 2 posts from 1 traveler on BootsnAll Travel Blogs ( [source](https://blogs.bootsnall.com/))\n9. 1999 · Contributors\n\nChris Heidrich, BootsnAll co-founder, has contributed to this guide since 1999, including dispatches from BootsnAll and AirTreks travelers",

        "url": "https://travel.gc.ca/destinations/egypt",
        "title": "Egypt travel advice",
        "description": "# Egypt travel advice  ![](https://travel.gc.ca/travel-voyage/assets/images/flags/48/EG.png)\n## Safety and security  ![](https://travel.gc.ca/images/icons/safety.svg)\n### Terrorism\n- airports and other transportation hubs and networks\n\n### South Sinai Governorate\n- always use main highways\n\n### Crime\nPurse snatching and pickpocketing occur most often in tourist locations and on the metro.\n\nAlthough isolated areas and night driving present the greatest threat, there have been reported incidents in daylight hours and in busy areas of Cairo.\n\n### Public transportation\nSafety standards for rail travel vary throughout Egypt. There have been major accidents in recent years, attributed to aging infrastructure, poor maintenance and human error.\n\nAvoid microbuses because of hazardous driving habits.\n\nOvercrowding and poor safety standards on ferries have caused accidents. Use reputable ferry operators.\n\n## Need help?\n### Local services\nIn case of emergency, dial:\n\n- medical assistance: 113\n- COVID-19 inquiries: 105",
